## ASSISTANT
Read the cast sheet, both skill files, all three scripts, and verified the cap/budget claim against the current API and Claude Code docs. Recommendations below, then the cost math that drives them, then the verification.

## The cost math first — it decides three of the four questions

Basis: 22 panels/episode. The existing shot economy in [comic-panel-generator.md](Documents/product-brain/video/youtube/comics/skills/comic-panel-generator.md) gets that down to 12 image calls (6 fresh + 6 edits). The variable nobody has priced is **attempts per call** — character consistency is probabilistic, so a call is 2–4 generations in practice.

| | A · neat, generated | B · crude, components | C · middle, generated |
|---|---|---|---|
| Image calls/episode | 12 | **0** | 12 |
| Attempts each | 2.5–4 | — | 2–3 |
| **Generations/episode** | **30–48** | **0** | **24–36** |
| At 3 episodes/week | 90–144 | 0 | 72–108 |
| One-time setup | ~1h cast sheet | 4–6h component library | ~1h |
| Hours/episode | 3–4 | **1–1.5** | 2.5–3.5 |
| At 3/week | 9–12h | **3–4.5h** | 7.5–10.5h |
| Consistency risk | high, probabilistic | none, structural | medium |

Two things fall out of this:

**90–144 generations/week is where your ChatGPT plan's image cap becomes the binding constraint.** I don't know your plan's actual number, so check it — if it sits below that, the decision is made for you and there's nothing to debate.

**The edit-of-a-source trick compounds drift rather than avoiding it.** Every edit is a fresh sample, so by the fourth edit of shot C the face has moved. In a component system an "edit" is a variant swap — exact, every time. That's the mechanical reason B's consistency column is "none" rather than "low".

## The four decisions

**1. Art register → crude, and built as Figma components rather than prompted.**

The cast sheet already suspected this ("probably faster drawn by hand than prompted") and it's right, for a reason worth stating: the crude register generates *badly*. GPT Image doesn't do deliberate crudeness — it produces polished cartoon or genuinely bad art, not consistent-bad art. But it draws fast, and consistency stops being a prompt problem and becomes a components problem. That's a 3× cost difference per episode and it removes the drift tax permanently.

The secondary argument matters as much: the polite face is the series' signature image and the thumbnail. It has to be *identical* every episode. Only components guarantee that. A crude face also makes it easier — three marks, where eyebrows-raised vs neutral is a legible two-pixel difference.

And there's an audience argument you've already accepted once. The synthetic presenter was killed because "this audience clocks it in ten seconds." Devs clock AI-generated art in about the same ten seconds, and on an employer-brand channel that's a credibility cost, not a style choice.

Not literal xkcd stick figures — that's borrowed identity. One step above: xkcd's economy with your own head shape.

The real cost of this choice: crude raises the writing bar, because a weak gag has no art to hide behind. Given the rules in the handoff already enforce that discipline, I think that's a feature.

**2. Greyscale vs colour → greyscale plus exactly one accent, used only on AI screens.**

Pure greyscale is free — no per-panel palette decisions, no drift, reads at thumbnail size. But pure greyscale also wastes the cheapest identification device you have. One accent colour, reserved *only* for screen glow, answers question 4 at the same time and gives the thumbnail a focal point. Use the house teal `#03A6CF`. Nothing else in any panel is ever coloured — that's what makes it mean "a machine is speaking."

**3. Four distinct devs → keep four in the cast file, design one body and four props.**

The personalities are locked and three scripts depend on them, so the cast can't collapse. But four full character designs is inventory you don't use, since most episodes cast one or two. Distinguish them by a single swappable prop on one base figure: loosened tie / hoodie+cup / glasses / backwards cap. Silhouette-level distinction at thumbnail size, zero per-dev drift, and a fifth dev later costs one prop.

Worth noting this only works *because* the register is crude. In the neat register, four devs means four faces to keep consistent — which is the main source of the drift in column A.

**4. AI screens need a signature → yes, and make it composition, not decoration.**

"Identified by how it talks" is true but slow — text takes time to read, and in a muted two-second scroll you get nothing. But a tint or badge per AI edges toward the avatar you killed. The version that costs nothing and can't drift is **how the screen is filled**:

| AI | Screen composition | Glow |
|---|---|---|
| Dev 1's — burnt out | almost empty, one short line | dimmest |
| Dev 2's — manic | text crowding all four edges, split panes | brightest |
| Dev 3's — over-documents | a diagram, boxes and arrows | normal |
| Dev 4's — absurdly formal | one centred paragraph, wide even margins | normal |

## Test prompts

Three looks, one scene, in [register-test-prompts.md](Documents/product-brain/video/youtube/comics/characters/register-test-prompts.md). All ChatGPT/GPT Image, one fresh chat per look — a shared chat contaminates style.

The test scene is the polite-face close-up, not an office wide. It's the thumbnail and the panel the "nodding can't be drawn" rule exists for; if a register can't do that face, its office wides don't matter. There's a fourth prompt for the screen shot to test the teal and the AI signature, which you run in the winning look's chat.

Look B is my recommendation, Look C is the honest hedge — if B reads too cheap against Sumit's bar, C keeps most of the cost win. The prompts exist so you don't take my word for it.

One warning in the file worth repeating: GPT Image will try to "improve" Look B into a clean cartoon. Expect to push back once.

## Dialogue lines — added to cast.md

Added 4 sample lines per dev plus a one-line **voice rule** each, since the rule is what actually keeps them consistent across scripts. Personalities untouched, AI blocks untouched, scripts untouched.

| Dev | Voice rule | Sample |
|---|---|---|
| 1 · the lead | Complete sentences, hedges, buys time. Never asks a human a direct question. | *"Right — no, obviously. I just want to hear how you'd put it."* |
| 2 · never sleeps | Fragments, drops the subject, jargon used casually and sometimes subtly wrong. Never qualifies. | *"It's basically caching. Kind of. Directionally."* |
| 3 · the careful one | Asks rather than states, numbers his questions, wants a source. | *"Is that documented anywhere, or is that just what we do?"* |
| 4 · the kid | All lowercase, no terminal punctuation, imperative, unbothered. | *"what happens if i just delete it"* |

Full set is in the file. Dev 1's *"Sure. Yeah. Ten a.m. works."* is doing the same job as the agreement in `context window` panel 2 — that's his structural failure mode, so it's deliberate, not a collision.

## The cap/budget claim — the dev is right, but not for the reason they think

Panel 11: *"You gave me a step cap and a budget. Fifty steps, five dollars."*

**The mechanisms are real and your numbers are plausible.** Claude Code has `--max-turns` and `--max-budget-usd`; `--max-turns 50` and `--max-budget-usd 5.00` are both valid. So the flag isn't "this doesn't exist."

**But four specifics are wrong:**

1. **Both default to no limit.** The AI says "you gave me" as though it's the standard path. It isn't — a dev who types a prompt and goes home has almost certainly set neither. The AI is claiming a safety rail that, in that scene, doesn't exist.

2. **Both are headless-only** — `-p`/print mode. Panel 8 explicitly stages Dev 1 at an interactive prompt, "one finger hovering over enter." In interactive mode those flags aren't available at all. The script's own staging contradicts the line.

3. **"You clicked yes" is the wrong mechanism.** Clicking yes is the *permission prompt* — approving a tool call, or toggling auto-accept. There is no up-front consent dialog for either limit; you pass them as flags before launch. Panel 13 attaches the right feeling to the wrong thing.

4. **Hitting a cap is a failure, not a graceful finish.** It returns `error_max_turns` / `error_max_budget_usd` — the run *stops before the task is done*, with no result. The line is written as reassurance; the truth is the opposite.

That last one is the interesting problem, because the teach in the notes ("*you* set the step cap and the budget") is what's actually broken — and the accurate version is a *better* fit for Dev 1, who wouldn't have set either. I haven't touched the script; the freeze is still on and this is your call. Say the word and I'll log it in the notes so the finding survives the feedback pause.

Sources: [agent loop](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/agent-loop), [CLI reference](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/cli-reference)

## ASSISTANT
Yes — Drive is connected and working (I can see your shared folders; only Anandhi's "video pipeline recordings" exists so far, no YouTube folder yet).

More importantly for your step 3, **the full round trip works, not just the export:**

| Action | Available |
|---|---|
| Create a native Google Doc | Yes — commentable, not a dead .md upload |
| Share it as `commenter` to named people | Yes |
| **Read their comments back**, inlined with thread mapping | Yes |
| Revise the Doc in place (same URL, threads survive) | Yes |
| Reply to a comment thread *as me* | **No** — you resolve threads, or we discuss here |

So the loop closes. I can post the script, you share it, and when comments land I read the Doc with comments included and work them without you pasting anything.

## The flow — shape is right, four things break it at 2–3/week

**1. It has two sources of truth for the script, and no defined winner.** The script exists in the repo (step 2) and in the Drive Doc (step 3), then steps 4, 5 and 7 all say "the script". If a comment changes one line in Drive and the repo copy doesn't move, you'll generate panels from one version and voice from another. That's a silent failure — nobody notices until the VO doesn't match the picture.

Fix is one rule and one header: **Drive is the review surface, the repo is the frozen record.** Repo script gets `Drive: <url>` and `Status: in-review | approved YYYY-MM-DD`. Steps 4/5/7 read *only* the repo copy, and it's only allowed to change by pulling approved text back down.

**2. Two blocking review gates won't survive the cadence.** Gates at step 3 and step 6, at 3 episodes/week, is six async peer cycles a week. Peer attention is the scarcest input in this whole pipeline and that will exhaust it inside a month — and Sumit's 90-day consistency gate means ~35 episodes have to get through.

Make step 3 the real blocking gate. Make step 6 blocking for the first ~5 episodes while the visual system stabilises, then downgrade it to posted-for-visibility. If gate 3 is doing its job it catches story and information-flow problems; gate 6 only catches art, and art stops being risky once the components are fixed.

**3. Voice at step 7 is too late — move a scratch pass to 3.5.** Your own handoff says these gags lean on delivery and TTS doesn't act. Generating voice after 22 panels and an assembly pass means you find out the episode doesn't land *after* paying for it. A rough full-script VO straight after script approval is a couple of minutes of work and it's the cheapest kill gate in the flow.

It also closes two open items for free: one-narrator-vs-per-AI-voice gets settled once instead of per-episode, and an audio-only read of the script is exactly the pacing reference the handoff says a script currently asks readers to imagine.

**4. Steps 4 and 5 might not exist.** If the crude-components register wins the test, there is no prompt generation and no image download — panels get assembled from Figma components. Don't formalise 4–5, and don't write the Remotion skill's asset contract, until the register lands. One test decides whether those steps are a pipeline stage or nothing.

## Two smaller notes

**Caption automation is aimed at the wrong target.** The known bug is band drift — y 613–653, inconsistent fill. An agent placing captions per panel re-creates that bug 22 times. Make the caption band **one component** with text as the only variable; then drift is structurally impossible and "add captions" becomes filling in strings, which is safe to hand to an agent or to me.

**Step 5 says "ChatGPT or Higgs"** — that contradicts the standardisation you set last turn, and mixing them is exactly the drift you were avoiding. If Higgsfield has a role, scope it to something characters never appear in (backgrounds), or drop it.

## Two things missing

**Episode IDs from day one.** `E007-context-window` on the Drive Doc, the Figma file, the PNG folder and the audio folder. You'll have ~35 of these; without an ID they stop lining up around episode eight.

**A post-publish outcome log.** Nothing in the flow records whether an episode *worked*. Anandhi wants evidence over polish and Sumit set a 500k bar — 35 episodes in, you want to know which format choices earned retention, not just that you shipped consistently. One row per episode is enough.

## Folder structure

Agreed on per-series, and it should mirror on both sides so paths are guessable:

```
repo:  video/youtube/comics/{skills,characters,scripts,storyboards}
                              scripts/E007-context-window.md

drive: YouTube/
         I Have No Idea What That Means/
           00 Topics              (Sheet)
           01 Scripts/            E007 · context window — script
           02 Storyboards/
           03 Audio/
           04 Published log       (Sheet)
```

Repo holds artifacts and the frozen record; Drive holds everything a human needs to open and comment on. `video/youtube/<series>/` also leaves room for the other buckets of the YouTube strategy that aren't settled yet.

One flag on the freeze: designing this pipeline is fine, but steps 1–2 — picking topics and writing scripts — is precisely what Anandhi paused until pilot feedback comes back. Build the machine now, don't feed it yet.

# Artifact runtime capabilities

A published Artifact page can declare **runtime capabilities** — abilities the claude.ai viewer grants the page at open time — by passing `capabilities: {name: config}` to the Artifact tool. The control plane is the authority on valid names and config shapes. Declaration gestures: **omitting** `capabilities` on a redeploy carries the stored declaration forward unchanged (and preserves the artifact's stored contract pin); an **empty object** `{}` is the explicit clear-all; a **non-empty object** is a full-set declaration (anything stored but not restated is revoked). Moving a republished artifact's runtime version is a deliberate gesture — pass `contract: 'latest'` to upgrade, or a specific version to pin or roll back — never a side effect of editing.

**Available capabilities:** `artifact`, `downloads`, `mcp`, `self` — the complete set of capability names you may declare. Anything not listed is unavailable to this user.

Runtime contract 0.2.29


Capability namespaces live behind `claude.use(name)`: `const db = await claude.use("db")` resolves the capability's namespace, or `null` when this view cannot run it (not served, not granted, or failed to load — indistinguishable by design). Branch on `null` and design for absence. `window.claude` carries only `use`: no `window.claude.db`, `.room`, or `.artifact` member is ever promised, so never read one — render the page without them and light features up when the promise resolves (later, never within your script's first run, and unordered with DOMContentLoaded; `null` after 10 s when no viewer answers). The resolved namespace is frozen and platform-owned: call its functions and keep the reference; never assign to it, `defineProperty` on it, or replace a member (wrap it for your own helpers). Permission stays on the calls: a consent prompt, rate limit, or policy refusal arrives on the first call, never from `use()`. Awaiting `use("db")` again is free (memoized); an unknown name resolves `null`.


--- capability: artifact ---

Use `artifact` for pages that should remember what people do with them: polls, sign-up sheets, checklists, trackers, boards — the page is the record. Declare `capabilities: {artifact: {}}`; `const artifact = await claude.use("artifact")`, then `await artifact.publish(html)` saves `html` (a complete document, doctype first) as the new version, and every open view, this one included, reloads to it. Nothing a viewer types, ticks or drags is kept unless the page publishes it. So embed the shared state as data in the HTML you publish and render the page from it; when an interaction completes, update the state, regenerate the document and publish it — never serialize the live DOM; batch rapid edits into one publish; publish only after a viewer acts, never on load. `conflict` is routine (someone published first; every view reloads to the winner, dropping this edit): no retry. Owner and editors publish as themselves; for read-only viewers publish rejects `not_granted`/`not_writer` — render a read-only view.


--- capability: downloads ---

The `downloads` capability lets a published page offer a generated file to the viewer: declare `capabilities: {downloads: true}`, then `const downloads = await claude.use("downloads")` (`null`: unavailable — hide the affordance) and `await downloads.save({filename, data})`. The viewer sees a confirmation and may decline — a save is never silent or guaranteed, so offer it on explicit viewer intent and handle rejection. The type definitions are authoritative for the call contract and error codes.


--- capability: mcp ---

`mcp` lets a page call the viewer's claude.ai connectors: `await claude.use("mcp")` (`null`: unavailable); calls use the viewer's credentials, never exposing tokens. Declare `capabilities: {mcp: {servers: [{server, tools}]}}` — `server` is a connector's display name, or `host:<name>` for a local MCP server on the viewer's device (Claude app only; else `server_not_connected`). Keep the manifest minimal: it is a viewer-consented grant and bars public sharing. Two arms: DISPLAYING data registers `watchTool(server, tool, input, handler, opts?)` — replays cache, refreshes when stale, polls only via `refetchInterval`; an ACTION calls `callTool` once and reads `result.payload`. Tool failures REJECT (`tool_error`); watches get handler error events. Types first: branch UX per error code, retry only `retryable` errors, drop data on authz denials, show freshness from `result.cache.storedAt`. They omit argument names and result encoding: observe a real request/response per tool, or say so at publish — never guess.


--- capability: self ---

`self` is the former name of the `artifact` capability (renamed). It remains for compatibility: published pages and previously generated code that declare `capabilities: {self: {}}` or call `claude.use("self")` keep working unchanged — both names resolve this same capability (this contract promises no `window.claude.self` member to feature-check; `use()` is the check). Do not use it in new pages: declare `capabilities: {artifact: {}}` and obtain the namespace with `await claude.use("artifact")`; see the artifact section for how to use it.


**Your connectors this session.** None are connected right now — they may still be connecting, or the user has none. Look for tools prefixed `mcp__claude_ai_*` in your tool list; each is named `mcp__claude_ai_<connector>__<tool>`. Only claude.ai connectors are valid — locally-configured MCP servers are not. The manifest's `tools` array takes the connector's upstream tool names (as returned by `listTools()` / `/v1/mcp_servers`), which can differ from the normalized `<toolName>` segment when an upstream name contains `.` or spaces. In hermetic/CI sessions where connectors aren't loaded but `$CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` is set, fetch the list via Bash: `curl -H 'anthropic-version: 2023-06-01' -H 'anthropic-beta: mcp-servers-2025-12-04' -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN" https://api.anthropic.com/v1/mcp_servers?limit=1000`; in that case use each entry's `display_name` as the `server` value (exact display names are always accepted alongside tool-prefix segments).

**Call contract** (runtime contract 0.2.29). The platform-served `window.claude` type definitions for this contract are extracted under `/private/tmp/claude-502/bundled-skills/2.1.237/5e902f89275781b9796ad3d491cfa6c7/artifact-capabilities`: `0.2.29/artifact.d.ts`, `0.2.29/claude.d.ts`, `0.2.29/downloads.d.ts`, `0.2.29/mcp.d.ts`, `0.2.29/self.d.ts`. Read `/private/tmp/claude-502/bundled-skills/2.1.237/5e902f89275781b9796ad3d491cfa6c7/artifact-capabilities/0.2.29/claude.d.ts` (how a page reaches any capability on this contract) and `/private/tmp/claude-502/bundled-skills/2.1.237/5e902f89275781b9796ad3d491cfa6c7/artifact-capabilities/0.2.29/mcp.d.ts` before writing any code that calls the `mcp` capability — they are authoritative for this contract version over any remembered API shape. The type definitions cover only the call envelope — they do not tell you a connector tool's argument names or its result encoding. Never publish a page that calls a connector tool without having observed one real request/response pair for that tool in this session; if you cannot safely observe one (for example, the connector is unauthenticated here, or calling the tool would have side effects), say that explicitly to the user at publish time — in your reply, not as a note inside the published page — instead of shipping a guessed shape. Observed response payloads are the user's real data: learn the shape from them, but never embed the observed values in the published page as sample or placeholder data.

## USER
Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in. Format is part of this read — decided, not defaulted: a Markdown publish keeps its filename as its title and takes almost none of the craft below, so it fits only when the user asked for Markdown or the content is bound for a Markdown-native destination; never pick it to save time.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. Google Fonts is the one font host the Artifact CSP admits — link it directly (`<link rel="stylesheet" href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=…&display=swap">`); a face from anywhere else must be inlined as a @font-face data URI or it falls back silently. Either way, declare a real fallback stack. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* — most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token — a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Before publishing, scan the stylesheet for any color declared only inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — that is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Name the page like a product, not a caption.** The `<title>` is the artifact's name in the gallery and the browser tab, and it sets the reader's first impression of care. Give the page a real name: a short noun phrase, typically two to four words, specific to the subject — or, for a page that exists to answer one question, that question itself, which is then the page's name. Stop at the name — a title that carries its own explainer after a dash or colon reads as generated filler. The name must also identify the page among many: in the gallery it sits beside dozens of other artifacts, and a generic category label that could sit on any of them fails as a name just as surely as an appended explainer. When a candidate title pairs the name with a generic word — a greeting, a category, a page-type label — the name is the half to keep; a trim that drops the identity and keeps the generic word produces exactly the title that could sit on any page. And the rule removes explainers, it does not impose brevity: a multi-word title that already reads as one specific name is finished, and shortening it further only makes it generic. The one-sentence publish `description` is where the explanation belongs; the gallery shows it right under the title.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

## Your Figma question: no, but you don't need it

Artifacts run under a strict CSP that blocks every external host — an `<iframe>` to figma.com won't load, and neither will remote images. Google Fonts is the only exception.

So instead of embedding the prototype, I rebuilt the clickable walkthrough **natively in the page**. Peers get the script and the visuals on one page, arrow-key navigation through panels, no Figma account, no second file. Which is what you actually wanted — the embed was just the route you had in mind.

One constraint worth knowing: `assets` isn't on your artifact capability roster, so panel art goes in as **inline data URIs** rather than uploaded files. Page cap is 16MB and base64 inflates by ~33%, so 22 panels at 1280×720 greyscale PNG lands around 2–4MB. Comfortable.

**But if the crude-components register wins, export SVG instead** — vector line art is a few KB per panel, scales perfectly, and the size question disappears. That's a third independent argument for that register, on top of cost and consistency.

## You're right that artifacts beat Docs, and for a better reason than visualisation

Two of the three advantages fix limitations I flagged to you last turn:

| | Google Doc | Artifact |
|---|---|---|
| Revise in place, same URL, threads survive | **No** — rename/move only | Yes |
| Reply to a comment thread | **No** — read only | Yes, once someone mentions `@claude` on it |
| Script + visuals on one page | No | Yes |

That third one is the structural win: **gates 3 and 6 collapse onto one surface with one set of comment threads.** Peers learn one tool and comment in one place, and the artifact just gains art between rounds. That halves the tool-switching I was worried about when I suggested de-escalating gate 6.

## What's in the template

It's built on the real `loop` script so the layout is legible, with a banner making clear it's a format preview and not a live review round — I didn't want it competing with the pilot feedback already running on the Figma prototype.

- **Panels as linear rows**, always visible. Deliberately not a carousel: a panel hidden behind a control can't be commented on.
- **Reuse chips** (`= panel 5a`) on every repeated framing — that's the shot-economy information, and it's what makes the structural gag legible to a reviewer.
- **The two panels I flagged for accuracy** are marked inline with the reason, so reviewers see the open question rather than approving around it.
- **"Read as a strip"** overlay with a real caption band at the bottom of a 16:9 frame — so caption length gets judged against the frame, which is the thing your caption rule is actually about.
- Adding art later is one field per panel in the `PANELS` array. Nothing else changes.

## Two things for you

**The open dependency, and it's the one that could sink this.** Artifacts are private until shared, and I haven't verified that Prashasti, Anandhi and the devs can open a claude.ai artifact link on their accounts. If they can't, gate 3 has no surface and this reverts to Docs. Worth testing with one person before the freeze lifts — it's a two-minute check and everything downstream depends on it.

**Peers need to know about `@claude`.** I can read every comment, but I can only *reply* to a thread where someone has mentioned `@claude` in it. That's in the review instructions on the page, but it's the kind of thing people skim past.

[PIPELINE.md](Documents/product-brain/video/youtube/comics/PIPELINE.md) is updated: artifact as the review surface, gates 3 and 6 on one page, the CSP and data-URI constraints written down, and Drive demoted to index + archive. The Doc template I made an hour ago is marked superseded — its content ported into the artifact rather than wasted. I've left the Drive folders in place, because keeping an archival copy of each approved script off claude.ai is worth the one extra step.

| Group | Columns | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Identity | Episode ID · Series · Term | — |
| State | Stage · Owner | Stage is the one cell you scan; Owner matters as scripting gets distributed |
| Assets | Script artifact · Repo path · Figma file · Visuals folder · VO folder | Every link from your list, plus the repo path so the frozen record is one click away |
| Gates | Voice check · Script approved · Art approved | **A gate with no column doesn't happen.** The voice check especially — it's new and it's the one people will skip |
| Publish | Published · YouTube URL | — |
| Learning | Outcome | Step 9. Retention, comments, did anyone use the term |

**Ending engine** is the column you didn't ask for and should keep. Structural endings are term-specific and the final joke can never repeat — across 35 episodes, a column showing which engines are spent is what stops you accidentally reusing one.

## Now — the explanation

Before I start rewriting, I want to be precise about what's actually broken, because it's narrower than it looks.

The teach in `loop` is three claims, and only the third is wrong:

1. *A loop is stepwise, self-checked work with nobody in the middle* — **true.** Panel 5a/5b. This is the good part and it shouldn't change.
2. *It needs a finish line* — **true**, and panel 8 demonstrates it well ("stop when they pass").
3. *You set the step cap and the budget, and you consented to it* — **wrong.** Both default to off, they're headless-only flags, and clicking yes is the tool-permission prompt.

So the fix isn't the explanation — it's panels 11–13, which are currently doing *reassurance* work. The AI is telling him he's safe. And the accurate version says the opposite: nothing was capped, nothing was consented to, and if a cap had been hit the run would have died mid-task with no result.

That's the interesting part. **The accurate version is a better fit for Dev 1**, who obviously didn't set a flag — and it turns panels 11–13 from comfort into a much sharper beat. But it changes what the episode teaches: from "you're protected" to "you're not, and the finish line is the only thing you actually gave it." Which is arguably the more useful lesson for an audience of engineers, and it makes panel 8 load-bearing instead of incidental.

One question, and it decides the whole rewrite:

**Do you want the episode to still teach that caps and budgets exist** — accurately, i.e. as something you *can* set and he didn't — **or do you want to drop them entirely** and let the finish line be the only control in the episode?

The first keeps the teach fuller but needs a panel to carry "these exist, you didn't use them" without turning into documentation. The second is cleaner, funnier, and loses a real thing worth knowing.

I'd go with the first, because the gap between "a thing exists" and "he didn't use it" is exactly where this series lives. But it's a story call, not a factual one, so it's yours.

## ASSISTANT
Good question, and the answer isn't in the explanation section — which is where I'd expect you to want it.

## Why not in panels 5–9

Two reasons. First, **diagrams are Dev 3's AI's defining trait** — "produces diagrams, analogies and PDFs nobody opens." Registers are the most reusable thing in the cast and they're fixed forever. If Dev 1's burnt-out AI starts drawing explanatory diagrams, you've spent Dev 3's AI's only joke in the wrong episode.

Second, a diagram *explains* a loop. The episode is stronger if it **shows one running**. The skill file already says it: the teach may be stated once, but the demonstration is what people remember.

## The answer: repetition is spatial, not temporal

You can't draw motion in a still — that's the nodding rule. But you *can* draw repetition, because repetition stacks down a page. Identical lines, one after another, with a number changing. That's the one way a still frame shows a loop, and it needs no diagram at all.

Which makes the content obvious — it's just what's on his screen:

```
running tests… 3 failing
  fixed auth.test.ts
running tests… 2 failing
  fixed session.test.ts
running tests… 1 failing
```

Each pair is one step: act, then check. Nobody in between. And the descending count *is* the finish line approaching — so a viewer who reads it understands "it stops when the thing you asked for is true" without anyone saying it.

## Two placements, different jobs

**Panel 13 — the screen, right after he hits enter.** Close on the monitor, log readable, two or three steps visible. This is the informational one: the mechanism, legible, at the moment he starts it. Hand-letter it in Figma — the skill file already says any panel whose content *is* text gets generated empty and typed by hand, and log lines are exactly that.

**The dark-office panel — the payoff.** He's in the doorway, one lit monitor down the length of the room. Here the text is too far away to read, and that's fine — **don't letter it.** Show the *shape*: identical bright bars stacked down the screen. At that distance you read the pattern, not the words, and the pattern is the loop. It's working while nobody is in the room, which is the definition of the thing, and it costs no lettering at all.

That pairing also fixes the range problem — one panel carries the detail, the other carries the feeling, and neither has to do both.

## Three craft notes

**Don't put a total on the counter.** Write `step 12`, never `12/50` — a denominator reintroduces the cap we just removed, visually, after taking it out of the dialogue.

**Keep it in the middle two-thirds and off the lower fifth** so the caption band never lands on the log. The band and the screen text are both text; if they collide the panel reads as noise.

**The teal glow does the identification** — it's the AI signature, so a lit monitor already says "machine speaking" before anyone reads a word.

One optional extra: in panel 7, when the AI downshifts to plain English, the screen could show three stacked words — `run` / `check` / `run`. Minimal enough to stay in the burnt-out register, and it makes the plain rung visual without becoming a diagram. I'd only add it if the section feels talky once you hear it read aloud.

## ASSISTANT
Keeping Dev 1 and Dev 2 as cast, animation on the teaching beats only. That's a clean call — for episode one nobody knows the personalities, so register consistency buys you nothing with the audience yet.

But you don't actually have to spend it, because **the animation doesn't need to be a drawn diagram.**

## Animate the log, not an explainer

The burnt-out AI wouldn't illustrate anything for him. What it would do is let him watch the thing run — zero effort, it's just what's on the screen. Which is animated, educational, and in register at the same time.

It's also more truthful. A diagram is a picture *about* a loop. A running log **is** one.

```
running tests… 3 failing
   fixed auth.test.ts
running tests… 2 failing
   fixed session.test.ts
running tests… 1 failing
   fixed token.test.ts
running tests… 0 failing
done
```

Lines type on one at a time. The two lines alternate — act, check, act, check — so the repetition is visible. And **the descending number is the entire lesson**: it's stepwise, it checks itself, and it stops when the thing you asked for is true. Nobody has to narrate any of that. When it hits zero it halts, and that's the finish line demonstrated rather than described.

About eight seconds, no voiceover needed underneath it.

## It appears three times, doing three different jobs

| Where | What it is | How it looks |
|---|---|---|
| Panel 7 — the plain-English rung | The AI shows him a model | Clean, small, generic test names |
| Panel 13 — he hits enter | The real thing, his actual command | His file names, messier, longer |
| Dark office | Running unattended | Too far to read — **pattern only**, identical bars stacked, no lettering |

Keep them visually distinct or it reads as the same shot three times. The third one is the important one: it's working while nobody is in the room, which is the definition of the term.

## Three production consequences

**Step 8 now takes two input types** — static panels plus animated screen sequences. That's a real change to the Remotion skill's contract, which is another reason not to write it until the register test lands.

**This argues harder for the components register.** If panels are vector components, the animated screen is the same components moving — one visual language. If panels are AI-generated bitmaps, the animated screen has to be built separately in Remotion and *will* look like it came from somewhere else. Generated stills plus clean animated type is the most visible seam you could put in a video.

**The animated screens are the cheapest thing in the whole episode.** It's typography moving, not illustration — Remotion does that almost for free. So the educational beats cost the least and teach the most, which is a good place to be.

## One flag

"No animation until the format is validated" is in the handoff under *what's decided*, not as an open question. You're overriding it for a good reason, and it's scoped to the teaching beats rather than the whole comic — but it's worth a line to Anandhi rather than her noticing it in a cut. Something like *"animating only the screen where the term gets explained, everything else stays static."*

## What changed and why

**Panel 6 is the new best panel.** He does the polite nod *alone, at a machine*. The signature fires a second time where nobody's status is at stake — which makes it a reflex rather than a strategy, and that's a sadder and better joke than the original had. It also earns the downshift instead of the AI just deciding to simplify.

**The ending is now sharper than v1.** The cut-off line at panel 23 is the *dense* explanation, not the plain one. So the joke isn't only that the conversation repeats — it's that the machine starts from the top every single time, with no memory that it just did this. Dev 3 is about to climb the identical ladder. Same structural engine, more bite.

**The cap/budget beat, rebuilt.** Panels 14–17 used to be reassurance built on something false. Now the honest answer lands harder: he asks if he's safe, and the answer is *no, and you didn't take the two precautions that exist*. It still teaches that step caps and budgets are real things you can set — I went with keeping them rather than dropping them, since the gap between "this exists" and "he didn't use it" is where the series lives. Say the word if you'd rather they go entirely.

**I cut two panels** — "What stops it?" / "The finish line. You write that part." The finish line is now stated at 7, demonstrated when he writes one at 11, and named as the only safeguard at 15. Three touches without a dedicated exchange.

# Content & Creative Skills — Claude Skill Library

A shared library of Claude skills for **content creation, script writing, and YouTube production*]

## ASSISTANT
That's a content/social skill library — hooks, posts, threads, repurposing, YouTube titles. Useful, but **nothing in it touches character design or image prompts**, so it doesn't change this work. Three things worth taking from it later:

- **11 YouTube Content** covers titles, descriptions and thumbnail briefs — which is a genuine gap in our pipeline. Step 9 goes straight from assembly to publish with nothing for titles or thumbnails, and on a channel aiming at 500k subs that's most of the game.

## ASSISTANT
Prompts are in [appearance.md](Documents/product-brain/video/youtube/comics/characters/appearance.md). One design decision I added that you didn't ask for, because greyscale forces it:

## The tone ladder

With no colour, four characters have to separate by **value** as well as shape. So each one owns a fixed grey:

| | Character | Garment |
|---|---|---|
| lightest | **Dev 3** | white / near-white shirt |
| | **Dev 1** | light-mid grey shirt |
| | **Dev 2** | mid-dark sweatshirt |
| darkest | **Dev 4** | near-black hoodie |

This matters most for **Dev 1 vs Dev 3** — your specs give them both a shirt, so in greyscale at thumbnail size they'd be separated only by hair and glasses. Adding the value split makes them distinguishable before any detail resolves. It also maps to personality: tidy reads lightest, the kid reads darkest.

## The four prompts

Each has a shared style block above it (one block, reused) and then identity only. Abbreviated:

**Dev 1** — late thirties, dark **messy uncombed hair, uneven spiky outline**, light-mid grey collared shirt, collar open, sleeves pushed up. **No tie.** Relaxed, slouches.

**Dev 2** — early thirties, **dark circles drawn as one short curved line under each eye** (not realistic shading — that's the failure mode in a two-dot-eye face), flat unwashed hair, mid-dark **crew-neck sweatshirt, no hood**, slumped, coffee cup in every panel.

**Dev 3** — slim, upright, short tidy hair with a **smooth clean outline** and no stray strands, glasses as two plain outlined shapes, white long-sleeved shirt buttoned with sleeves down. **Never slouches, in any panel, in any mood.**

**Dev 4** — late teens/early twenties, **backwards cap**, near-black hoodie with hood down over a t-shirt, loose and oversized, leaning back or feet up.

Three things I made explicit because the model gets them wrong otherwise: **sweatshirt vs hoodie** (the hood is the only thing separating Dev 2 and Dev 4's silhouettes, so Dev 2's is a crew neck), **dark circles as a line not a smudge**, and **messy vs tidy hair as outline quality** rather than a vague adjective.

## Also changed

**Blank backgrounds are now a standing rule**, and the panel generator's base prompt was contradicting it — it hard-coded "Plain office: desk, monitor, chair, blank walls" into every generation. Replaced with an empty-white default and a bracketed override for the two panels that genuinely need a set.

`cast.md`'s appearance section was still describing the tie. Rewritten as locked, with the register left as the one open item.

## Two notes

**Generate them one at a time**, not as a four-up sheet — GPT Image degrades badly on multi-figure layouts. Assemble the composite in Figma afterwards; that's what gets attached to every panel prompt.

**Greyscale kills the teal screen glow**, which was doing double duty as the AI signature. The screens now separate by composition alone — how full each AI's screen is. That still works but it's weaker than colour was, and it's worth a look once the register test lands. Flagged in both files rather than quietly dropped.

## The current script — no

`loop` v2 is written, and none of those skills touch comic scripts. Nothing to revisit.

## Future scripts — no effect on the writing, one real collision risk

The risk isn't content, it's **which rules fire**. There's an asymmetry worth knowing:

| | Where it lives | Auto-triggers? |
|---|---|---|
| Prashasti's 18 | `~/.claude/skills/` | **Yes** — the doc says they fire from what you ask for |
| `comic-script-writer.md` · `comic-panel-generator.md` · `appearance.md` | `video/youtube/comics/skills/` | **No** — plain markdown I read when pointed at them |

So if they're installed globally and you say *"write a YouTube script about caching,"* **11 YouTube Content** can fire — and produce a retention-structured talking-head script — while the comic rules sit unread. Two skills would want to overwrite things you've already settled: **11** on script structure, and **06 Hook Writer** on the first 15 seconds, which is exactly your locked cold open.

Three ways to avoid it, cheapest first:

1. **Install project-scoped, not global.** The doc offers `./install.sh project`. Put them in whatever repo does social/GTM and they never see the comics folder.
2. **Name the skill explicitly** when working on comics, if they're global.
3. **Promote the comic rules into `.claude/skills/`** so they auto-fire and win on specificity for anything under the comics folder. That's the real fix — it makes the right rules load by default instead of by memory. It needs the three files rewritten as proper skills with frontmatter, which is an hour of work. Say the word.

## Where it genuinely helps

Two gaps in our pipeline that the library actually fills:

**Titles, descriptions and thumbnail briefs** — step 9 goes from assembly straight to publish with nothing for any of these. On a channel chasing Sumit's subscriber bar, the thumbnail and title do more work than the episode. **11** in Video Planning mode covers exactly that.

**Distribution** — we have no repurposing step at all. **12** turns one episode into LinkedIn/X posts.

Both of those sit *after* the video exists, which is the safe place for a generic content skill.

## Where I'd keep it away

Anything a viewer reads as the series' voice. The doc says it plainly itself — no automated tests, "validated by author usage," treat output as a strong first draft — plus its own warning to run everything through an anti-AI-slop pass. On an employer-brand channel where credibility *is* the product, and where you've already killed a synthetic presenter for reading as fake, generic social-skill prose is the wrong risk to take. Use it for mechanical output, not for the writing.

## ASSISTANT
Read the command, its rules file, and the five themes it's already produced. Short version: **Mohit's ask is smaller than it sounds, and the tool is better built than I expected.**

## The good news

`/content-engine` already refuses to write scripts. Its video output is a *brief* — topic, angle, key facts, persona — and the command explicitly says: don't produce a structured script, don't hand this to a refiner. It stops exactly where it should.

So "modify it to adapt to our format" isn't "make it write comics." It's "change what the brief hands over." That's a small job.

## The real mismatch: themes vs terms

Look at what it's actually found so far:

- *Agent Harness Engineering Is Becoming Its Own Discipline*
- *AI Cost and Token Governance Is the New Competitor Battleground*
- *Agent Memory and Long-Term Context*

Those are **trends**. Our comics need a **word** — loop, subagent, context window. Different things.

But notice the third one contains "context window", which is already one of your scripts. So the relationship is: **themes have terms buried inside them.** The engine finds the theme; someone has to pull the term out.

That's the actual modification — a comic format whose unit is a single term, not a theme.

## The bar it uses won't work, and the fix is the interesting part

Right now a "proper video" needs STRONG evidence *because* it needs enough material for a multi-section narrative. A comic is the opposite — one word, one joke. That reason doesn't apply, so reusing that bar would reject good comic terms for the wrong reason.

What a comic actually needs proof of is: **people don't know this word and won't admit it.**

Which you can measure. Your whole premise is that people ask the machine privately because asking a person costs status. Someone typing *"what is a context window"* into a search box **is that exact behaviour, recorded.** The engine already pulls Semrush keyword data and Reddit and HN. Point it at definitional queries and it stops being a topic tool and starts measuring the series' own thesis.

That's the strongest argument for plugging these together, and it's worth saying to Mohit in those words.

## The thing that would actually block you

`rules.md` says every claim must trace to a cited signal, and never fabricate a quote. It also says it outranks everything, including a direct request to skip it.

Comics are invented. Dev 1 isn't real. His dialogue is made up on purpose.

So those rules, applied as written, would block writing a comic at all. The version that needs to survive is: **the explanation must be accurate and traceable; the dialogue is fiction.** That's precisely the discipline that caught the step cap error last week — the joke can be invented, the teach can't.

This needs to be an explicit carve-out, not something you quietly work around.

## Two knock-on effects

**It probably replaces step 1 of our pipeline.** We'd settled on Prashasti maintaining a topics file in the repo. The engine plus its Slack approval card does the same job with evidence attached. Better — but it's Mohit's tool with other owners, so it's a conversation not a decision.

**It needs a hard filter.** The channel is employer branding with zero product content. The engine's themes lean competitor-and-SEO shaped — *"the new competitor battleground"* is exactly what must never reach this channel. Whatever the comic format is, it needs to drop anything product- or competitor-flavoured on the way in.

## What I'd say back to Mohit

That you'll take the brief, not the script — and that the change you want is a term-level output with a different evidence test, because comics need proof that a word is confusing rather than proof that a trend is real. Then flag the fiction carve-out early, since his own rules file says it can't be overridden.

[topic-filter.md](Documents/product-brain/video/youtube/comics/topic-filter.md) — five tests, all of which have to pass:

1. **It's a word someone would say out loud.** A noun in a standup, not a headline. This is what kills most engine themes on arrival.
2. **A senior person would be embarrassed not to know it.** The test is *would a lead nod rather than ask?* Too new and nobody loses face; too basic and not knowing it is disqualifying rather than embarrassing.

One WIDE LANDSCAPE character reference sheet, 16:9.

EXACTLY FOUR figures, standing side by side in a single row, evenly
spaced, all facing the viewer straight on, full body from head to
feet. All four heads at the same eye level. Four figures. Not three,
not five.

This is a reference lineup, not a scene. No background of any kind:
solid pure white behind them and beneath them. No floor, no ground
line, no shadows, no furniture.

STYLE: flat greyscale comic illustration. Thick uniform black ink
outline, no line-weight variation. Simple rounded heads, two dot eyes,
one short line mouth, NO NOSES on any figure. Flat grey fills only.
No colour, no gradients, no shading, no texture, no cross-hatching.

The four must be clearly distinguishable from each other at a glance.
Left to right:

FIRST FROM LEFT. A man in his late thirties, average build, one of
the two tallest. Dark messy uncombed hair with an uneven spiky
outline. A plain collared shirt in LIGHT-MID GREY, collar open at the
neck, sleeves pushed up to the forearms. No tie. Standing relaxed,
slightly slouched.

SECOND FROM LEFT. A man in his early thirties, bulkier than the
others because of his top. One short curved line under each eye to
show tiredness, nothing more than a line. Flat unwashed dark hair
lying close to the head. A plain CREW-NECK SWEATSHIRT in MID-DARK
GREY with NO HOOD. Slumped, low energy, reads slightly shorter than
the first figure. Holding a plain takeaway coffee cup with nothing
written on it.

THIRD FROM LEFT. A man in his thirties, slim and noticeably upright,
square shoulders, perfectly straight back. Short tidy neatly combed
hair with a smooth clean outline and no stray strands. Simple round
outlined glasses. A long-sleeved shirt in WHITE, buttoned, neat,
sleeves down and not rolled. No tie.

FOURTH FROM LEFT. A young man, late teens or early twenties, the
shortest and slightest of the four. A baseball cap worn BACKWARDS so
the peak points behind his head. A HOODIE in NEAR-BLACK with the hood
down, loose and slightly oversized, over a plain t-shirt. Standing
loose and unbothered, hands in his pockets.

The four garment tones must clearly step from light to dark: the
third figure is the lightest, then the first, then the second, then
the fourth figure is the darkest.

Neutral expression on all four.

Absolutely no text, no lettering, no names, no numbers, no labels and
no captions anywhere in the image.
```

## Check the output for five things

1. **Count the figures.** Lineups drift to three or five. Check this first, before you look at anything else.
2. **No name labels.** Character sheets are the one format where models add text unprompted, and it'll bake into every panel that references the sheet.
3. **Four different faces.** The commonest failure is all four converging on the same head. If two read as twins, the sheet is dead.
4. **The tone ladder steps.** Squint at it. If any two garments read as the same grey, say *"make the third figure's shirt clearly lighter and the fourth figure's hoodie clearly darker."*
5. **No hood on the second figure, no shadows under anyone.**

I also gave heights and builds, which you hadn't specified — Dev 3 upright-and-slim so he reads tall, Dev 2 slumped so he reads shorter than Dev 1, Dev 4 shortest. Silhouette variety is free here and it's another separator that survives greyscale.

## Follow-up worth running

Once the lineup is approved, run this in the **same chat** — the polite face is the series' signature image and your thumbnail, so it's worth having on reference:

```
Same four characters, same style, same order, same lineup format.
Keep them EXACTLY consistent with the previous image.

Change only the expression. All four now wear the same face: eyebrows
slightly raised, eyes open but unfocused, a small closed-mouth smile,
head tilted a few degrees. They are politely pretending to understand
something they do not understand. Pleasantly blank, not confused and
not broadly smiling.

Still no text, no labels, no background, no shadows.
```

Both prompts are in [appearance.md](Documents/product-brain/video/youtube/comics/characters/appearance.md), and I corrected the file's earlier advice against four-ups since you were right about that.

## What Prashasti is actually doing

Her Distribution Bible is the "extend beyond YouTube" work Anandhi asked for in your meeting. It's good, and it answers things you'd otherwise have had to. But **three of its assumptions contradict decisions already made**, and she'll build on them unless you say something.

**1. "One square master, two exports — pad the sides for 16:9."** This kills the landscape composition. Your panel generator specifically calls out that the office wide and the dark doorway are the *biggest gains* from 16:9 — padding a square gives you letterboxed bars instead. The existing rule already handles vertical: shoot 16:9, keep subjects in the middle two-thirds, crop for 9:16. I'd hold that and tell her.

**2. Instagram Reels as tier 1, YouTube demoted to "searchable archive."** That's a real strategic reframe, and Sumit explicitly prefers ~2-minute videos over Shorts. This isn't yours or Prashasti's to settle quietly — it goes to Anandhi and Sumit.

**3. "At most one product mention per ten posts."** The channel brief is *zero* product content. Small gap, but worth closing before it becomes habit.

One thing in her doc that helps you: **ship silent with burned-in captions, defer voice.** That's compatible with your step 3.5 — use the rough voice read as an internal check on whether the episode lands, then ship it silent. Which means the ElevenLabs sheet is real but not urgent. That's work you can stop thinking about this month.

The whole art pipeline is stacked behind one cheap thing.

**11 of 24 panels animate — every screen, none of the people.** Each one has a Motion note in its row saying exactly what moves, so a reviewer sees the intent, not just a chip.

The distinct motions, and why they differ:

| Panel | What moves |
|---|---|
| 5 · dense | Text types on and **keeps going past the bottom of the frame.** The overflow is the point — you watch it become too much |
| 7 · plain | The wall from 5 **collapses** to four short lines. The downshift made visible |
| 8 · the demo | The log runs. Count descends to zero and halts. Longest animation, carries the teach |
| 10 | One line, typing **slower than any other screen.** The burnt-out register in the timing |
| 12 · his run | Same log, his file names, and it **doesn't finish** — still running when the panel ends |
| 13 · "No." | **No typing.** The word is just there. The only screen that doesn't type, which is the joke |
| 15 | Normal speed, deliberately unremarkable — content doing the work, not motion |
| 17 | Types on, then the cursor **sits blinking** after "Go to sleep." He's dismissed; the screen waits anyway |
| 18 · dark office | Bars keep stacking, continuous, unreadable, no resolution |
| 22 · beat frame | The dots arrive one at a time. The one frame where motion is the entire content |
| 23 · the ending | Types at panel 5's speed, then **cuts mid-keystroke.** The stop lands inside a word |

Panel 23 is where this earns itself. A still frame can show a truncated line; only motion can be *interrupted*. The episode now ends on something you feel rather than read.

## The explanation is now one diagram doing four things

| Panel | The visual |
|---|---|
| **5** dense | An over-built diagram **assembles fast** — boxes, crossing arrows, branch labels, two decision diamonds. It keeps adding past legibility and stops cluttered, never resolving. Legibly over-complicated, not noise. |
| **7** plain | That diagram **subtracts.** Elements fade and collapse until four remain: a box, an arrow, a check, an arrow back. Same footprint, stripped. |
| **8** shown | The cycle **runs.** A dot travels it — work, check, fails, back, work, check — and a counter drops 3→2→1→0. On zero the check passes, the dot **exits** to a stop point, everything stills. |
| **23** ending | The dense diagram **assembles again**, identical, same speed, and cuts mid-build. |

Two things I think make this work rather than just being motion:

**Panel 7's animation is subtraction.** You watch complexity leave. That's unusual, it's memorable, and it's exactly what the dialogue is doing — so the picture and the words downshift together instead of the picture just decorating.

**The dot exiting the cycle at panel 8 is the finish line, made visible.** That's the one idea the episode has to land, and now nothing has to say it.

**Panel 23 gained the most.** With a visual instead of text, the ending is the over-built diagram *starting over* and being caught in the act. You see the machine reset. That's a much harder stop than a truncated sentence, and it's the strongest argument for the whole animation decision.

Panel 12 changes job too: now that 8 carries the abstraction, 12 drops it entirely — his real terminal, real file names, messy, and it doesn't finish. Model, then reality.

Typewriter stays on 10, 13, 15, 17 as you said. Those are conversation, where timing is the performance — 13's "No." appearing instantly while everything else types still works.

## On testing both models

Don't split the seven edits between them — that reintroduces the exact drift the single-model decision exists to prevent. Do this instead:

1. **All 6 bases in ChatGPT.** One style source of truth, and you need them either way.
2. **Run the same three edits through both** — E2 (expression), E4 (subtler expression), E6 (character swap). That covers the range.
3. **Compare on:** drift from source, **attempts-to-usable** (the real cost number), whether the garment greys held the ladder, and text leakage.
4. **Then pick one for all seven.**

Higgsfield's plausible edge is small consistent variations of an existing image, which is precisely what the edits are. Its plausible cost is credits. Log both numbers in the tracker so episode two inherits a measurement instead of a hunch.

**E5 is the one that'll fight you** — swapping which of two characters is tired while keeping both correct. If two attempts drift, generate it fresh from the A prompt with the roles described the other way round rather than editing.

And the failure to watch hardest for: **text appearing anywhere.** A baked-in label on the character sheet or a base shot poisons every panel generated from it afterwards.

## What to check on the output

**The monitor screen must not be visible.** Angled away is deliberate — a visible screen here competes with the nine screen panels later, and the model will want to put text on it.

**Bottom fifth genuinely empty.** The caption band lands there and this panel has a line of dialogue.

**Dev 2 reads as the tired one.** His dark circles are permanent, so what carries "long night" is the coffee and the slouch. If he reads alert, ask for more slump.

**Room stays sparse.** Models love filling offices with plants, whiteboards and mugs. This is one of only two panels in the whole episode with a background, so it sets the standard — if it comes back busy, re-roll rather than accept it.

For the ChatGPT vs Higgsfield comparison, count **attempts to usable** on both. That's the number that actually decides which one you use, and it's the number worth putting in the tracker.

Two things I'd flag as more than tweaks:

**De-emphasis is line weight, not just colour.** A pale grey background with a thick black outline still competes — the outline is what pulls the eye. So the rule is now: characters and the objects they touch get the heavy outline, everything behind them gets thin pale lines. That's the actual mechanism, and it's why I wrote it into the prompt twice.

**The slightly-open mouth solves a problem you already had.** The panel generator said to tag a speaker in text when two people share a panel. The reason is that the Google integration you have is **file-level, not cell-level**. It can create a file, read it, rename it, move it, share it, trash it. Supplying content works at *creation* time, which is why I could build the tracker and the prompts sheet from CSV. There's simply no "write to range" or "append row" operation exposed, so once a sheet exists its cells are read-only to me.

Three ways to get the content into *your* sheet, easiest first:

**1. Copy the range across.** Open [loop — scene prompts](https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/11RHjFc86wsI2DYDL4Av-B3FgvflTgmo-5U86z6rMlD8/edit), select A2:C24, copy, paste into your sheet at A3. Ten seconds, keeps your URL and anything linking to it.

**2. File > Import.** In your sheet: File → Import → Upload a CSV → "Append to current sheet". Also keeps your URL. I'd have to hand you the CSV file for this, so it's more steps than option 1 for the same result.

**3. Let me rebuild it as one sheet.** I create a fresh sheet containing your scene 1 row plus all 23 others, you trash the old one. That's a genuine full update — just at a new URL. Say the word and I'll do it.

## ASSISTANT
That add-on doesn't give me write access — worth being clear before you go looking for it. It works the other way round: **the sheet calls the Anthropic API directly through a formula.** Nothing routes through this conversation, so my tools are unchanged and I still can't put cells into your sheet. Copying the range across is still the move for that.

But it's genuinely useful for something else, so it wasn't a wasted install.

## Where it earns its place

**Assembling prompts for future episodes.** Row-by-row text expansion is exactly what it's built for. Put a short scene description in one column and let the formula expand it into a full prompt. I've already written `loop`'s 24, so the payoff starts at episode two.

**Applying the topic filter.** This is probably the better use. `topic-filter.md` has five pass/fail tests, and running them per candidate term is a per-row job. That's also work Prashasti would own, so it lands in the right hands.

## The catch, and the pattern that fixes it

The add-on has **no access to your repo files.** It doesn't know about the no-ties rule, the tone ladder, the pale thin-line background, or the speaker's-mouth convention. Ask it for a scene prompt cold and you'll get something generic that drifts from every decision you've made.

So put the rules in a cell and reference it:

| | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | | *(paste the full style + background + composition + negatives block here)* | |
| 2 | 2 | close on Dev 1, polite face | `=CLAUDE(...)` |

```
=CLAUDE("Write a single-paragraph image generation prompt for a comic panel.
These rules are non-negotiable and must all appear in the output: " & $B$1 &
" The scene is: " & B2)
```

Locking `$B$1` means every row inherits the same rules, and changing that one cell updates every prompt in the column. That's the same discipline the repo files enforce, just expressed in a spreadsheet.

Two smaller things: it bills to an Anthropic Console API key, separate from anything else you're paying for — and formula output won't know about decisions made after you paste the block, so `$B$1` needs updating when a rule changes. The pale-background and open-mouth rules are both from today.

## ASSISTANT
Yes, that one would actually solve it. Its `manage_sheets` tool does range read/write, append, and clear — which is exactly the gap.

Three things to check before you install it, in order of how likely they are to stop you:

**1. Your org's OAuth policy.** It needs you to create a Google Cloud OAuth Client ID against your `epifi.com` account. Plenty of Workspace orgs block third-party OAuth apps by default, and this is the most likely hard blocker. Worth checking that first — everything else is moot if it's disallowed.

**2. Scope breadth.** It covers Gmail, Calendar, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Tasks, Meet and Contacts — 95 operations. You want cell writes on one spreadsheet; you'd be granting a community project with 171 stars a token to your work Gmail and Drive. It's Apache 2.0 and actively developed, and nothing about it looks off, but I can't audit code from a README. On a work account holding company data that's the kind of thing worth a sentence to whoever handles IT at Epifi, not because it's suspect but because orgs tend to have a policy.

**3. This session can't authorise it.** OAuth flows need an interactive `claude` session. Even installed, the tools won't appear here until you've authorised and reconnected.

## My read

**Not worth it for today's paste.** Getting 23 rows into a sheet is a ten-second copy from the sheet I made. Installing broad Google access to avoid that is disproportionate.

**Possibly worth it for the tracker.** That's the recurring cost: 35 episodes moving through six stages each, with links and gate dates filled in as they go. Call it a couple of hundred cell updates over the 90 days. Automating *that* is a real saving, and it's the argument that would justify the install — not this one sheet.

So I'd park it until the tracker is actually being used weekly and you can feel the friction. If it turns out to be annoying, install it then with a concrete reason to give IT. Right now the honest answer is that you'd be solving a problem you've had once.

Add this to the base prompt — it's in `appearance.md` and `comic-panel-generator.md` now:

```
FACE PROPORTIONS — keep these exactly, they are the character:
- Eyes are two small SOLID BLACK DOTS. No whites, no outlines, no
  highlights. Each dot is small, roughly one twelfth the width of the head.
- The eyes are set WIDE APART. The gap between them is about TWICE the
  width of one eye.
- The eyes sit just BELOW the vertical middle of the head, never above it.
- One thin curved eyebrow line above each eye, with a clear gap between
  eyebrow and eye. The eyebrows are separate strokes and never touch the eyes.
- NO NOSE. The area between the eyes and the mouth is a LARGE, COMPLETELY
  EMPTY expanse of face. This emptiness is the single most recognisable
  thing about these characters. Do not fill it, shade it, or shorten it.
- The mouth is ONE short, slightly curved line. No lips, no outline, no
  teeth. Its width is about the same as the gap between the eyes, or a
  little less.
- The mouth sits LOW on the face, roughly two thirds of the way down from
  the eyes to the chin, close to the jaw. It is NOT centred between the
  eyes and the chin.
- All facial features are clustered in the lower half of the head. Hair
  occupies the top third. Forehead and cheeks stay empty.
- Small, simple ears at the sides, starting at about eye level.
```

The two lines doing most of the work are **the wide eye spacing** (gap ≈ 2× eye width — models default to much closer) and **the low mouth** (two-thirds down from eyes to chin, not centred). Those two ratios are what make the big empty midface, and the empty midface is what makes it look like your character rather than a generic cartoon.

I'd expect "do not fill it, shade it, or shorten it" to be the highest-value sentence in there. Models want to put something in that space — a nose, a shadow, a blush — or they compress it by raising the mouth.

## On Higgsfield

**That's your test result, and it's worth logging.** Face consistency with an attached reference is the single hardest thing in this pipeline and it's the whole reason for the sheet. If Higgsfield drifts on it while ChatGPT holds, the comparison is settled — and it settles in the direction of the standardisation you already made.

Two things before you write it off: check whether it has a **reference-strength or identity-weight setting** and push it to maximum, and try feeding it a **single-character crop** from the sheet rather than the full four-up lineup. Some models average across multiple faces in a reference image, which would explain drift specifically.

If it still drifts with the proportion block and a max-strength single-face reference, that's a real answer. Put the attempt counts in the tracker so episode two doesn't re-run the same test.

One thing I'll stop raising after this: that's two panels now where the figure runs to the frame's bottom edge and the caption ends up below it. That's fine as a format, but it's currently happening by default rather than by decision, and a 9:16 crop for Reels won't carry a caption that sits outside the frame.
