## ASSISTANT
You're right and my version was worse. A line that changes every episode isn't an outro — it's just an ending. Three fixed outros gives you recognition *and* variation, which one line can't.

And the part I'd underline, because I don't think it's incidental: **the buckets do the brand work, not the dialogue.** Every episode is quietly filed under speed, cost or quality — the same three axes the product runs on. Nobody is ever told that. But the shape of the whole body of work matches the shape of what you sell. That's brand architecture rather than brand messaging, and it survives the zero-product-content brief completely intact.

## The three lines

I'd keep them as siblings — same rhythm, same delayed deadpan, same "something of mine is gone." The missing noun *is* the metric:

| bucket | outro |
|---|---|
| **cost** | "…hey. What happened to my AI credits?" |
| **speed** | "…hey. What happened to my morning?" |
| **quality** | "…hey. What happened to my code?" |

Six words, same beat, same blank face, after the stamp. After four or five episodes people start clocking which one they're about to get — that's a small pleasure and a reason to sit through the end card.

`loop` keeps the line it already has. Nothing in the current cut changes.

## The one real problem, and how I'd settle it

**Most terms straddle two buckets.** That isn't a flaw in your model — speed/cost/quality *is* the fast-cheap-good triangle, so terms naturally touch more than one:

- `subagent` — speed (parallel) or cost (n agents, n bills)
- `context window` — quality (it forgot) or cost (you pay for the whole window every turn)
- `prompt caching` — literally both, by design
- `rate limit` — speed (blocked) or cost (tier)

If you try to classify by what the term *technically* is, you'll argue about it every time.

So classify by **where the joke pays off, not where the term sits.** `loop` touches all three — but the ending is credits burning, so it's cost. `context window` becomes quality if the punchline is the AI forgetting him, and cost if the punchline is paying for 200k tokens of nothing. The bucket follows the ending.

That's not a workaround. It slots straight into test 4 of the topic filter, which already says you have to know your ending before you commit. Now the ending also picks the outro. One decision, two jobs.

**Second thing worth watching:** track the mix. If eight of your first ten terms land in cost, you're back to one outro. Worth a column in the episode tracker so it stays roughly even.

## One defence to have ready

If someone asks whether sorting topics into product buckets is product-led content creeping in: speed, cost and quality are so universal to engineering that essentially every term fits one. The bucket constrains nothing about what you can cover — it only decides which of three endings you use. It costs you no editorial freedom, which is exactly why it's safe.

I've written the bucket rule into [topic-filter.md](Documents/product-brain/video/youtube/comics/topic-filter.md) and added `BUCKET` to the handover block, so Prashasti gets it with the rest of the criteria rather than needing you in the loop. The three lines in there are marked provisional — say the word once you and she have settled them and I'll lock them in.

where would the term harness come in?

## ASSISTANT
Good test case, because `harness` is the most ambiguous term you'll hit — technically it sits in **all three** buckets. A good harness makes agents faster, cheaper *and* better; that's literally the pitch. So the technical read gives you nothing.

Which is exactly where the rule earns its keep: **the bucket follows the ending, not the term.** So the real question is what the ending is.

## Two viable endings, two different buckets

**The double meaning is the gift here.** "Harness" in normal English *restrains* — a dog, a toddler, a climber. An agent harness *enables*. The dev hears "we need a better harness" and assumes it's about putting the thing on a leash, when it's about the scaffolding that makes it work at all. That misunderstanding is the episode.

From there:

**Ending A — he leashes it.** He "improves the harness" by restricting the agent, and it comes back useless. Payoff is about output → **quality** → *"…hey. What happened to my code?"*

**Ending B — the inversion.** He learns the agent has a harness, and realises he has one too: standup, PR template, review, the ticket. His is worse. Payoff is about process eating his day → **speed** → *"…hey. What happened to my morning?"*

## I'd take B

Three reasons:

1. It's the funnier one, and it turns the machine's constraint onto the humans — which is the series' actual engine. He's the one in the harness.
2. **Quality is going to be your crowded bucket.** `eval`, `hallucination`, `flaky test`, `code review` all land there naturally. Speed is the one that'll be thin, and `harness` is a strong candidate to fill it.
3. "What happened to my morning?" lands *harder* here than anywhere else — the joke is that his own scaffolding is what ate it.

The teach still lands, because it lives in the middle, not the ending: the leash misunderstanding is the setup, the middle explains that the harness is everything around the model, and the inversion is the button. Bracket, not layer.

## Your weak test is #3

`harness` is hard to *show*, because a harness is negative space — it's defined by what it isn't. Worth solving before committing.

The best answer I have: **same prompt, run twice.** Left screen, bare model, one shot, wrong. Right screen, same model — same version badge, visibly identical — wrapped in the harness: the loop, the tool call, the retry, the check. It lands. Same model, different everything.

That's drawable on the monitor plate you already have, and it teaches the real thing — the model isn't the product, the scaffolding is.

## One flag before you pitch it

**Tetriz has a feature called Harness.** The term is industry-standard, not yours, so an episode on it isn't product content by any reasonable reading. But out of every available term, this is the one that shares a name with something you sell — which makes it the one where "isn't this just marketing?" is easiest to say, and hardest to answer without sounding defensive.

Not a reason to kill it. A reason not to make it episode two. Run three or four episodes with no overlap at all first, so the pattern is established before you touch the one with the collision.

If you want, I can write `harness` up properly in the four-field handover format the topic filter expects — term, why they won't ask, the teach, candidate ending — and add the bucket reasoning as the worked example in the filter file, so Prashasti has something concrete to pattern-match against.

"Yo" is an opener — it announces a beginning, so the loop reads as a restart rather than a consequence. Without it, the outro's diff stat flows straight into the request and the seam disappears.

## `context window` — rebuilt around cost

You're right that it didn't work. The "About what?" ending needed the viewer to reconstruct that the AI's window had filled and silently dropped his first question — that's a lot of inference for a button, and if it doesn't land you have no ending at all.

Cost fixes it, and it gives you a **better teach**: most people think the context window is about forgetting. The thing they don't know is that **you re-pay for the entire conversation on every single message.** That's non-obvious, genuinely useful, and it's the actual reason long chats get expensive.

**Bucket: cost.** Ending engine: self-demonstration — the question "how do I make this cheaper" is the most expensive message he sends all night.

| # | Direction | Line |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | A Slack thread on screen. Two messages. | **DEV 2** "root cause was the context window. cost us four grand" / **MANAGER** "nice find. [Dev 1] — can you take the team through this tomorrow? 10am" |
| 2 | Close on Dev 1. Polite face. | **DEV 1** "Sure." |
| 3 | Same framing. Thought caption. | *I have no idea what that means.* |
| 4 | Night. His desk, lamp on. Leaning toward the mic. | **DEV 1** "What's a context window?" |
| 5 | The screen. | **AI** "It's how much I can hold at once." |
| 6 | Dev nodding, already typing. | **DEV 1** "okay so it remembers everything" |
| 7 | Screen. | **AI** "It re-reads everything. Every message you send, I read the whole conversation again. You pay for all of it, every time." |
| 8 | Dev, not listening, already typing. | **DEV 1** "cool cool" |
| 9 | Dev, typing. | **DEV 1** "and what counts toward it" |
| 10 | Screen. | **AI** "Everything. Including this." |
| 11 | **WIDE — the chat window.** Scrolled enormously far, scrollbar a sliver. Timestamps `9:14pm` → `3:40am`. Hundreds of messages. Down the right edge, a per-message cost, **quietly getting bigger every line.** | — |
| 12 | Dev, wrecked, satisfied, rubbing his eyes. | **DEV 1** "okay. i think i've got it. how do i make it cheaper" |
| 13 | Screen. | **AI** "Start a new chat. Go to sleep." |
| 14 | Dev stopping. Hand off the keyboard. | **DEV 1** "and lose all this?" |
| 15 | Screen. | **AI** "Yes." |
| 16 | Close on Dev 1. The polite face — aimed at nothing. | — |
| 17 | Stamp, holding alone. | `CONTEXT WINDOW · CLEARED` |
| **18** | **OUTRO.** Stamp holds. Below it, small and lit: `Credits remaining: 0` | **DEV 1** "…hey. What happened to my AI credits?" |

**Panel 11 is still the hero image**, but it's doing different work now — the cost column climbing down the right edge is the mechanism made visible, and it pays off panel 7 without anyone restating it.

**The ending is the trap, not the forgetting.** The only fix costs him the entire night he just spent. That's a real dilemma engineers actually have, and it's a stronger button than a confused machine.

**No loop-back here.** Two of three episodes cutting to the top makes it a template, and your topic filter says structural endings have to be term-specific. Subagent earns it. This one's ending is the dilemma, and cutting away from it lets him off the hook.

**⚠ Flag for a spot-check:** "you pay for all of it, every time" is true by default, but prompt caching reduces the re-read cost where it's enabled. Worth one engineer confirming the wording before it's drawn — this is the exact class of claim that broke v1 of `loop`.

## Speed terms — I'd do `rate limit`

Three worth considering, but this is the one:

**`rate limit`** — someone hits 429 and everything stops. What nobody knows is that the limit is usually **per organisation, not per person** — so you're blocked because a colleague is running something. That's the series' engine exactly: the machine's constraint landing on the wrong human. Sayable in any standup, senior people absolutely nod rather than ask, the screen is trivial (a 429 and a retry countdown), and the clock outro is native — he's been waiting. Zero product adjacency.

The other two, briefly:

**`streaming`** — the best *teach* of the three: streaming doesn't make anything faster, it makes it feel faster. Total time identical. The ending writes itself — he ships a progress bar and everyone says the app got quicker. Funny and slightly damning. Second choice.

**`cold start`** — cleanest pure-speed mechanism and the easiest to draw, but weaker on test 2. A lot of seniors do know this one, so less status is at risk in asking.

I'd skip **`cycle time`** for now even though it has the biggest real insight. It's a management metric, which puts it closest to what you sell — same caution as `harness`.

## The speed outro

I proposed "…hey. What time is it?" — and having sat with it, it's the weak one. Two problems: a clock at 4am reads as *he stayed up late*, which is just Dev 2's personality, not a speed failure. And `loop` and `context window` are both already set at night, so it'd collide.

Better:

| bucket | outro | on screen |
|---|---|---|
| **cost** | "…hey. What happened to my AI credits?" | `Credits remaining: 0` |
| **speed** | "…hey. What day is it?" | `In Progress · 6 days` |
| **quality** | "…hey. How many files did it change?" | `1,847 files changed · 0 reviewed` |

**Days, not hours.** It implies far more loss, it can't be mistaken for "he worked late," and it covers every speed failure rather than just long-running jobs — being blocked, waiting, re-planning, arguing with your own config. A ticket card reading `In Progress · 6 days` is the number, and it's the most recognisable object in engineering.

It also lands hardest on the episode I'd do next: `AGENTS.md` ends with `git blame` telling him he wrote the rule that's blocking him, and then — "what day is it?" — Thursday. He started Monday.

All three now have the same shape: a question, and a number that's obviously wrong.

## On `Skills` — you're right, I was too quick

I dropped it because "hoarding inventory" is a character joke with no clear damage. But your point about it working across other people's work changes the term, not just the framing.

If the joke is **reuse** rather than hoarding, it's a real speed episode: he writes a skill, everyone else's work gets faster, and nobody knows he wrote it. The team's velocity went up and his didn't. That's the series' inversion again — the benefit lands on everyone except the person who built it — and it's a genuinely nice thing to say about engineering on an employer-brand channel.

That's a different episode from the one on the slate, and a better one. Worth telling Prashasti the mechanic should change.

## On taking it slow — agree, and there's a reason beyond taste

Three things point the same way.

**The register test still hasn't been run.** Three prompts, one sitting, about thirty minutes — and every art decision is stacked behind it. Nothing should be produced at pace until that's settled, because rushing means locking a look you didn't choose.

**The pilot is out for feedback and iteration is paused** — Anandhi's call. Shipping episode two before that feedback lands means you'd be repeating mistakes you haven't heard about yet.

**And the format needs a run, not a sprint.** The three outros only start reading as a system somewhere around episode four or five — that's when a viewer clocks that there are exactly three and starts recognising which one is coming. Six episodes at one a week gets you there in six weeks. Six episodes rushed in two gets you there with six episodes you're embarrassed by, on a channel whose entire product is credibility.

The honest counter is Anandhi's "don't wait for approval to move." But moving isn't the same as publishing — you can have four scripts written, the register locked and the bucket running order set without a single new video going out. That's what "slow" should mean here: the pipeline stays busy, the feed doesn't.

It's earned twice. The whole point of a skill is that you write it once so the thing never happens again — so ending on **the thing happening again** is the mechanism failing, not just a callback. ## Three things wrong, in order of size

**1. Nobody wants anything.** Your own rule. Dev 3 asks a polite question. Dev 2 suggests a file. Dev 1 writes it. At no point is anything threatened. Compare what the other three have:

- `loop` — he wants to go home
- `context window` — he presents at 10am
- `subagent` — Dev 4 wants to make more of himself, and is delighted

`skill` has "he'd prefer fewer interruptions." That's a preference, not a want. There's no reason he does this at 4am instead of next Tuesday, so the viewer never leans in.

**2. The ending has no reversal.** Panel 19 lands him exactly where panel 3 left him — same face, same situation. Structurally it's a loop; emotionally nothing turned. And panel 13, "the release takes eleven minutes," tells the audience something **they already worked out from the timestamps in panel 11.** A button has to reveal, not confirm. "You've had this the whole time" works because it's new information.

**3. The term fails two of your five tests.** This is the one I should have pushed harder on instead of writing around it.

- **Test 2** — would a senior be embarrassed not to know it? Barely. "Skill" sounds like plain English. A lead can ask "what do you mean, a skill?" and lose nothing. The entire series runs on *asking costs status*, and this word doesn't charge anything.
- **Test 3** — show the mechanism. A skill is a document. My hero image is a long document, which is the same image as `context window`'s long chat, and the weaker version of it.

Three out of five. Your filter says four is a rejection.

## If you want to keep it, there's one fix

Give him a real reason to fear being asked: **the interruptions are where he gets caught.** Every time someone asks him something, he risks exposure. So he writes the file to stop being asked — not to save time, to stay safe.

Then the reversal writes itself, using Dev 3's locked line:

> **DEV 3** "I read the whole thing. It doesn't say."

He spent five hours to stop being asked, and now he's the owner of the document, so he gets asked *more* — and about the one case he didn't cover. Documentation doesn't end questions, it generates better ones.

That fixes want and reversal. It does not fix test 2 or 3.

## What I'd actually do — `AGENTS.md`

Same bucket, visible loop, and it passes the tests `skill` fails. The spine:

1. He's blocked. Needs to ship today. The agent refuses to do the thing.
2. **AI** "That's not allowed." He argues. It won't move.
3. **AI** "You told me not to."
4. He opens the file. Finds the rule. Doesn't recognise it.
5. `git blame` — **his name, four months ago.**
6. He deletes the rule. Ships. Relief.
7. Then adds a new rule, so this never happens again.
8. Stamp → clock → **cut to panel 1: blocked.**

Why it's stronger on every axis:

- **The want is immediate** — he's blocked right now and has to ship.
- **The reversal is a real reveal** — the antagonist is himself, and neither he nor the audience sees it coming until the blame annotation.
- **Test 2 holds** — "it's in AGENTS.md" sounds like a convention you should already know, so asking costs you.
- **Test 3 is a single specific frame** — one line of config, and beside it his own name and a date. That's a much better hero image than a long document, and it's cheap to draw.
- **The loop is the mechanism, not a shrug** — he closes it himself, in panel 7, by doing the exact thing that caused it. That's the joke `skill` was reaching for and couldn't get to.

Want me to write it out properly? And say if you'd rather I take the rescue pass at `skill` instead — it's your call whether the term earns the slot.

## ASSISTANT
Yes — and it fixes both things I flagged, better than my rescue did.

**The want is real and it's funnier than a work deadline.** "I have a dentist appointment at 6" is mundane, unarguable, and gives every beat a clock running under it. A 10am presentation is dread; a dentist appointment is just *sad*, which suits this series better.

**And the reversal is now genuine.** He opens complaining about too many md files and closes having made forty more. He causes the exact problem he was trying to solve. That's the thing my version didn't have — mine ended where it started emotionally, yours ends with him having *made it worse*.

**The teach improved too.** You're teaching the anti-pattern — the failure mode of skills is proliferation, and the fix is factoring shared rules into one place. That's more useful than "here's what a skill is," and it's the part nobody tells you.

## Three things to fix

**1. The button should be an image, not the AI congratulating him.**

Your beat 11 has someone saying "now you can use these to make things faster." Don't say it — **show the file tree, bigger than it was in beat 1.** Panel 1 asked "why are there so many md files?" and the last frame answers it. Same shot, three times the files. No dialogue.

That's the reveal doing the work. A line of dialogue explaining the irony deflates it.

**2. Beat 5 needs actual content.** "Explanation from AI" is the middle of the episode, and the middle is where the straight teaching lives. Something like:

> **AI** "A file that says how you do something. I read it when the task matches. You write it once."

Short and flat. Then beat 9's better advice, also short:

> **AI** "You could put the shared rules in one fi—"

**3. The casting is the real decision, and it has a trap.**

"skillzzz" and "yeah not listening I'm busy making" is **Dev 4's** voice — lowercase, no fear, reads nothing. But Dev 4 is `subagent`'s protagonist, and `subagent` is *also* unbounded enthusiasm producing proliferation. Ten agents making their own agents; forty skill files. Same engine, same character, two episodes.

So either:

**Give it to Dev 1 and change the motor from delight to compulsion.** He doesn't make forty skills because it's fun — he makes them because every skill is one more thing he'll never have to admit he doesn't understand. Same escalation, same file tree, but it's driven by fear instead of joy. That also keeps the dentist appointment funnier: a lead with a 6pm dental appointment is better than the kid with one.

Cost: you lose "skillzzz", which is a good line but isn't in his register.

**Or keep Dev 4 and accept the overlap** — in which case the two episodes need clearly different buttons, and I'd want `subagent` and `skill` far apart in the running order.

I'd take Dev 1. The engine differentiation matters more than the line.

## One rule this forces us to change

We'd said the outro is always spoken by Dev 1 so the wording stays identical. This breaks that — "hey what time is it?" only lands because we know about the 6pm appointment, so it has to come from whoever has the appointment.

That's fine, and simpler: **the outro is spoken by the episode's protagonist.** The line is short and neutral enough to sit in any of their mouths, which was the original worry. And it's better — the outro should indict the person we just watched, not a bystander.

## The tightened spine

1. Wide: his screen, a file tree of md files. **DEV 1** "why are there so many md files? this is taking forever. i've got a dentist thing at 6"
2. Dev 2 passing, not stopping. **DEV 2** "use a skill. saves you all that"
3. Close on Dev 1. Polite face. **DEV 1** "Right — a skill. Yeah."
4. Same framing, mouth closes. *I have no idea what that means.*
5. **DEV 1** "what's a skill"
6. **AI** "A file that says how you do something. I read it when the task matches. You write it once."
7. **DEV 1** "so i can make one for anything i do twice?"
8. **AI** "Yes."
9. **DEV 1** "ok. make one for the deploy. and the migration. and the release notes. and—"
10. **AI** "You could put the shared rules in one fi—"
11. **DEV 1** *(over it, typing)* "No — no, this is good. This is the right call."
12. **WIDE — the file tree.** Same shot as panel 1. Three times the files. Timestamps down the side.
13. Close on Dev 1. The polite face, aimed at nothing.
14. `SKILL · CLEARED`
15. **OUTRO.** Clock. **DEV 1** "…hey. What time is it?"

Fifteen panels, and beat 12 carries the whole joke without a word. Want me to write it up properly and replace what's on disk?

## ASSISTANT
**B, clearly.** Two reasons, and they're the two that matter most.

**The skill actually works.** In A he only ever *hears* what a skill is. In B he uses one and it saves him time — so the mechanism is demonstrated, not described. Your own script rule says the demonstration is what people remember, and A never demonstrates anything.

**And B has a turn.** It goes good → bad: he saves time, then spends more than he saved. A is monotone — downhill from the moment he starts typing, with no peak.

Everything else follows from those:

| | A | B |
|---|---|---|
| Inciting line | "use a skill" — abstract advice | "just use my skill file from the repo" — how it actually happens |
| Teaches the social property (someone else's skill works for you) | no | **yes, and it's the best thing about skills** |
| Dev 3 | absent | offering something good, and being right — the series needs one of those |
| Requires a character to ignore good advice | yes | no |

B also gets Dev 3 right in a way the series needs. He's the one who reads every doc and wants a spec — of course he's written a proper skill file and committed it. And he's *helpful*, which he never gets to be otherwise.

## Three things B needs

**1. It has no button.** It goes straight from him spamming requests to the clock. Every other episode has one: `loop` restarts, `subagent` says "bruh", `context window` says "About what?" B needs something between the spamming and the card.

The AI is the place for it — flat, factual, no opinion:

> **AI** "You have created eleven skills. None of them have been used."

**2. Move the clock after the card.** You have it before. If "what time is it?" is the episode's button, the outro slot is empty — and the outro only becomes a signature if it's the last thing every time. Order should be: button → card → clock.

**3. The title beat is missing.** "rightt a skill" implies it, but the thought caption *I have no idea what that means* is the series' name. It goes in as its own panel, right after.

## The casting — I'd move it off Dev 4

Not a taste call. **`subagent` is already "Dev 4 over-applies a capability until it multiplies."** Ten agents spawning their own agents; eleven skill files. Two of your first five episodes, same shape, same character.

Give it to **Dev 1** and the episode gets a motive nothing else in the slate has:

He's handed Dev 3's skill file. It works. He's relieved — and then immediately needs to be the *author* rather than the borrower, because having needed help from the careful one is a small humiliation he can't sit with. So he stays late writing skills nobody asked for, and misses the thing at 6.

That's not enthusiasm, it's **status recovery** — which is exactly his character, and it's the only episode that would run on it. It also makes the button land harder: eleven skills, none used, because he didn't write them for anyone.

If you'd rather keep it light and fast, Dev 4 works — just put `subagent` and `skill` far apart in the running order, and make sure the buttons don't rhyme.

One small thing either way: keep "**I need to be somewhere at 6**" if it's Dev 4 — he wouldn't explain himself. If it's Dev 1, make it the dentist. He'd over-explain, and "dentist" is funnier because it's pathetic.

Want me to write B up properly with Dev 1 and replace `skill.md`?
